# PREFACE TO THE SECOND EDITION

**Christianity as Mystical Fact** was the title given by the author to this work, when, eight years ago, he gathered into it the substance of lectures delivered by him in 1902. The title indicated the special character of the book. In it the attempt was made, not merely to represent historically the mystical content of Christianity, but to describe the origin of Christianity from the standpoint of **mystical contemplation**. <note>The author uses "mystical contemplation" not as a dreamlike state, but as a disciplined form of perception or "spiritual seeing."</note> Underlying this intention was the thought that at the genesis of Christianity mystical facts were at work which can only be perceived by such contemplation.

It is only the book itself which can make clear that by “mystical” its author does not imply a conception which relies more on vague feelings than on “strictly scientific statements.” <note>The author is likely responding to contemporary critics who viewed mysticism as the opposite of rational science.</note> It is true that “mysticism” is at present widely understood in the former

<summary>The author introduces the second edition of his work, explaining that he views the origins of Christianity through the lens of "mystical facts" which require a specific, disciplined form of spiritual contemplation to understand.</summary>
<keywords>Christianity, Mystical Fact, mysticism, spiritual science, 1902 lectures, historical origins, contemplation</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's defense of his terminology, specifically addressing the scientific validity of mystical inquiry as established in the opening of the preface.</meta>

# iv Preface to the Second Edition

sense, and hence it is declared by many to be a sphere of the human soul-life with which "true science" <note>The author uses "true science" (German: *Wissenschaft*) here to refer to the rigorous, systematic study of reality, which he argues should include spiritual as well as physical phenomena.</note> can have nothing to do. In this book the word "mysticism" is used in the sense of the representation of a spiritual fact, which can only be recognised in its true nature when the knowledge of it is derived from the sources of spiritual life itself. If the kind of knowledge drawn from such sources is rejected, the reader will not be in a position to judge of the contents of this book. Only one who allows that the same clearness may exist in mysticism as in a true representation of the facts of natural science, will be ready to admit that the content of Christianity as mysticism may also be mystically described. For it is not only a question of the contents of the book, but first and foremost of the <term>methods of knowledge</term> <note>Refers to the specific cognitive techniques or "spiritual perceptions" the author claims are necessary to observe non-physical realities.</note> by means of which the statements in it are made.

Many there are in the present day who have a most violent dislike to such methods, which are regarded as conflicting with the ways of true science. And this is not only the case with those willing to admit other

<summary>The author defines his use of the term "mysticism" as a rigorous method of spiritual observation and argues that Christianity can be studied with the same clarity as natural science.</summary>
<keywords>mysticism, spiritual science, epistemology, Christian mysticism, scientific method, spiritual life</keywords>

# v Preface to the Second Edition

interpretations of the world than their own, on the ground of “genuine knowledge of **natural science**,” <note>The author refers to the late 19th-century debate where "natural science" was often seen as the only valid form of knowledge, excluding any spiritual or "mystical" inquiry.</note> but also with those who as believers wish to study the nature of Christianity.

The author of this book stands on the ground of a conception which sees that the achievements of natural science in our age must lead up into **true mysticism**. In fact, any other attitude as regards knowledge actually contradicts everything presented by the achievements of natural science. The facts of natural science itself indeed cannot be comprehended by means of those methods of knowledge which so many people would like to employ to the exclusion of others, under the illusion that they stand on the firm ground of natural science. It is only when we are prepared to admit that a full appreciation of our present admirable knowledge of nature is compatible with genuine mysticism, that we can take the contents of this book into consideration.

The author’s intention is to show, by means of what is here called <term>mystical knowledge</term>, <note>In this context, "mystical knowledge" refers to a systematic, disciplined method of experiencing spiritual realities directly, rather than through vague feelings or blind faith.</note> how the source of Christianity prepared its 

<summary>The author argues that modern scientific achievements, when correctly understood, naturally lead to a mystical understanding of the world rather than contradicting it. He asserts that his study of Christianity is based on this harmony between scientific rigor and spiritual insight.</summary>
<keywords>natural science, mysticism, Christianity, epistemology, harmony of science and faith, mystical knowledge</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues the sentence from the previous page, concluding the author’s thought that Christianity established its own foundation within the spiritual traditions that preceded it.</meta>

## vi Preface to the Second Edition

own ground in the mysteries of pre-Christian times. In this pre-Christian <term>mysticism</term> <note>In this context, "mysticism" refers to the "Mystery" schools of antiquity—secret religious societies where initiates sought direct experience of the divine.</note> we find the soil in which Christianity throve, as a seed of a quite independent nature. This point of view makes it possible to understand Christianity in its independent being, even though its evolution is traced from pre-Christian mysticism. If this point of view be overlooked, it is very possible to misunderstand that independent character, and to think that Christianity was merely a further development of what already existed in pre-Christian mysticism. Many people of the present day have fallen into this error, comparing the content of Christianity with pre-Christian conceptions, and then thinking that Christian ideas were only a continuation of the former. The following pages are intended to show that Christianity presupposes the earlier mysticism just as a seed must have its soil. It is intended to emphasize the peculiar character of the essence of Christianity, through the knowledge of its evolution, but not to extinguish it.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;It is with deep satisfaction that the author is able to mention that this account of the...

<summary>The author clarifies that while Christianity grew out of the "soil" of ancient pre-Christian mystery traditions, it remains a unique and independent spiritual force rather than a simple continuation of what came before. He argues that understanding this evolutionary background actually highlights the distinct nature of Christianity.</summary>

<keywords>pre-Christian mysticism, spiritual evolution, essence of Christianity, mystery schools, religious history</keywords>

## vii Preface to the Second Edition

nature of Christianity has found acceptance with a writer who has enriched the culture of our time in the highest sense of the word, by his important works on the spiritual life of humanity. Edouard Schuré, author of *The Great Initiates* <note>original: "Les Grands Initiés"</note>,¹ is so far in accord with the attitude of this book that he undertook to translate it into French, under the title, *The Christian Mystery and the Ancient Mysteries* <note>original: "Le mystère chrétien et les mystères antiques"</note>. It may be mentioned by the way, and as a symptom of the existence at the present time of a longing to understand the nature of Christianity as presented in this work, that the first edition was translated into other European languages besides French.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The author has not found occasion to alter anything essential in the preparation of this second edition. On the other hand, what was written eight years ago has been enlarged, and the endeavour has been made to express many things more exactly and circumstantially than was then possible. Unfortunately

# Christianity as Mystical Fact

---

## I

### POINTS OF VIEW

Natural Science <note>The author uses the term "Naturwissenschaft," referring to the rigorous, empirical study of the physical world that dominated European thought in the 1800s.</note> has deeply influenced modern thought. It is becoming more and more impossible to speak of spiritual needs and the life of the soul, without taking into consideration the achievements and methods of this science. It must be admitted, however, that many people satisfy these needs, without letting themselves be troubled by its influence. But those who feel the beating of the pulse of the age must take this influence into consideration. With increasing swiftness do ideas derived from natural science take possession of our brains,

<summary>This introductory page explores the profound impact of modern natural science on contemporary thought and the necessity of reconciling scientific methods with the internal needs of the human soul.</summary>
<keywords>Natural Science, spirituality, modern thought, soul, methodology, intellectual history</keywords>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page continues the introductory argument from the previous section, moving from the intellectual dominance of natural science to its emotional impact and the rise of scientific materialism.</meta>

# Christianity as Mystical Fact

and, unwillingly though it may be, our hearts follow, often in dejection and dismay <note>The author refers to the existential anxiety felt by late 19th-century thinkers who feared that scientific progress might strip the world of its spiritual meaning.</note>. It is not a question only of the number thus won over, but of the fact that there is a force within the method of natural science, which convinces the attentive observer that that method contains something which cannot be neglected, and is one by which any modern conception of the universe must be profoundly affected. Many of the outgrowths of this method compel a justifiable rejection. But such rejection is not sufficient in an age in which very many resort to this way of thinking, and are attracted to it as if by magic. 

The case is in no way altered because some people see that true science long ago passed, by its own initiative, beyond the shallow doctrines of force and matter <note original="Kraft und Stoff">This refers to the title of a famous 1855 book by Ludwig Büchner, which became a foundational text for German scientific materialism.</note> taught by materialists <term>materialists: thinkers who argue that physical matter is the only reality and that consciousness is merely a byproduct of biological processes</term>. It would be better, apparently, to listen to those who boldly declare that the ideas of natural science will form the basis of a new religion <note>A reference to "Monism," a movement popularized by Ernst Haeckel, which sought to replace traditional Christian faith with a "religion" based on evolutionary biology and natural laws.</note>. If these ideas also appear shallow and superficial to one who knows the deeper spiritual needs of humanity, he must nevertheless take note of them, for it is to them that attention is now turned,

<summary>This page describes how the "force" of scientific methodology has captured the modern mind, leading to a materialistic worldview that challenges traditional spiritual needs and even attempts to establish a new "religion" based on natural laws.</summary>
<keywords>materialism, force and matter, scientific method, monism, spiritual needs, secularization</keywords>

->Points of View<-

# Christianity as Mystical Fact

and there is reason to think they will claim more and more notice in the near future.

Another class of people have also to be taken into account, those whose hearts have lagged behind their heads. With their reason they cannot but accept the ideas of natural science. The burden of proof is too much for them. But those ideas cannot satisfy the religious needs of their souls,—the perspective offered is too dreary. Is the human soul to rise on the wings of enthusiasm to the heights of beauty, truth, and goodness, only for each individual to be swept away in the end like a bubble blown by the **material brain**? <term>material brain: the philosophical view—prevalent in the late 19th century—that consciousness is merely a byproduct of physical brain matter, meaning that when the body dies, the "soul" ceases to exist</term>. This is a feeling which oppresses many minds like a nightmare. 

But scientific concepts oppress them also, coming as they do come with the mighty force of authority. As long as they can, these people remain blind to the discord in their souls. Indeed they console themselves by saying that full clearness in these matters is denied to the human soul <note>The author is describing a state of "cognitive dissonance" where people live in two separate worlds: one of cold scientific facts and one of warm religious hope, unable to bridge the gap between them.</note>. They think in accordance with natural science so long as the experience of their senses and the logic of their **intellect** demand it, but they keep to the religious 

<summary>This page describes the psychological conflict of the modern individual who intellectually accepts the laws of natural science but finds the resulting materialistic worldview spiritually suffocating. It highlights the "nightmare" of viewing the human soul as nothing more than a temporary byproduct of the physical brain.</summary>
<keywords>natural science, soul, materialism, intellect, spiritual discord, 19th-century philosophy, religious needs</keywords>

# Christianity as Mystical Fact

sentiments in which they have been educated, and prefer to remain in darkness as to these matters,—a darkness which clouds their understanding. They have not the courage to battle through to the light.

There can be no doubt whatever that the habit of thought derived from **natural science** <term>natural science: specifically the 19th-century movement toward "Positivism," which argued that only things that can be measured or observed by the senses are real</term> is the greatest force in modern intellectual life, and it must not be passed by heedlessly by any one concerned with the spiritual interests of humanity. But it is none the less true that the way in which it sets about satisfying spiritual needs is superficial and shallow. If this were the right way, the outlook would indeed be dreary. Would it not be depressing to be obliged to agree with those who say: 

> “Thought is a form of force. We walk by means of the same force by which we think. Man is an organism which transforms various forms of force into **thought-force**, an organism the activity of which we maintain by what we call ‘food,’ and with which we produce what we call ‘thought.’ What a marvellous chemical process it is which could change a certain quantity of food into the divine tragedy of <note>The author is likely referring to Shakespeare’s *Hamlet* or a similar masterpiece. He is highlighting the absurdity—from a spiritual perspective—of viewing high art as merely the end product of a digestive process.</note>

<summary>This page explores the limitations of a purely scientific worldview, arguing that reducing human thought and art to mere "chemical processes" or "thought-force" leaves the soul's deepest needs unsatisfied.</summary>
<keywords>natural science, thought-force, materialism, spiritual needs, intellectual life, chemical process</keywords>

->Points of View<-

Hamlet.” This is quoted from a pamphlet by **Robert G. Ingersoll** <note>Robert G. Ingersoll (1833–1899) was a famous American orator and political leader known as "The Great Agnostic" for his defense of science and reason over religious dogma.</note>, bearing the title, *Modern Twilight of the Gods*. It matters very little whether such ideas find only limited acceptance in the world at large. The real point is that countless people feel forced by the system of **natural science** <term>natural science: here referring to the 19th-century scientific worldview that sought to explain all phenomena, including human consciousness, through purely physical and chemical laws</term> to adopt an attitude toward world-processes that aligns with these views, even when they believe they are not doing so.

It would certainly be a dismal outlook if natural science itself forced us to accept the creed proclaimed by many of its modern prophets. It would be most dismal of all for someone who has gained, from studying the content of natural science, the conviction that within its own domain, its way of thinking is valid and its methods are undeniable. Such a person is forced to admit that, regardless of how much people may argue over specific details—though book after book may be written and thousands of observations gathered regarding the **struggle for existence** <term>struggle for existence: a central concept in Darwinian evolution describing the competition between organisms to survive and reproduce</term> and its significance, or the power or weakness of **natural selection** <term>natural selection: the process by which biological traits become more or less common in a population due to the consistent effects of those traits on the survival and reproduction of their bearers</term>—natural science itself is moving in a direction which,

<summary>This page addresses the tension between the modern scientific worldview and spiritual needs, noting how 19th-century materialism—epitomized by thinkers like Robert Ingersoll—threatens to reduce human culture to mere physical processes.</summary>
<keywords>Robert G. Ingersoll, materialism, natural science, struggle for existence, natural selection, world-processes, scientific method</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the argument regarding the philosophy of science. It transitions from a critique of modern materialistic "prophets" to an analysis of the actual methods used by great scientists like Darwin and Lyell, suggesting that their observational approach—rather than a purely reductionist chemical one—is the proper model for studying the spiritual life.</meta>

# Christianity as Mystical Fact

within certain limits, must find acceptance in an ever-increasing degree.

But are the demands made by **natural science** <term>natural science: here referring to the late 19th-century scientific movement that sought to explain the world through physical laws and empirical evidence</term> really such as they are described by some of its representatives? That they are not so is proved by the method employed by these representatives themselves. The method they use in their own sphere is not such as is often described, and claimed for other spheres of thought. Would **Darwin** <note>Charles Darwin (1809–1882), the English naturalist who established the theory of evolution through natural selection.</note> and **Ernst Haeckel** <note>Ernst Haeckel (1834–1919), a German biologist who was a primary proponent of Darwinism and evolutionary theory in continental Europe.</note> ever have made their great discoveries about the **evolution of life** if, instead of observing life and the structure of living beings, they had shut themselves up in a laboratory and there made chemical experiments with tissue cut out of an organism? Would **Lyell** <note>Sir Charles Lyell (1797–1875), a Scottish geologist whose work provided the geological timeframe necessary for Darwin's theory of evolution to be plausible.</note> have been able to describe the development of the crust of the earth if, instead of examining strata and their contents, he had scrutinised the chemical qualities of innumerable rocks? Let us really follow in the footsteps of these investigators who tower like giants in the domain of modern science. We shall then apply to the higher regions of **spiritual life** <term>spiritual life: the inner world of human consciousness and soul-experience, which the author argues should be studied with the same rigorous observation used in biology or geology</term> the methods they have used in the study of nature. He need only do as science does, but he must not allow himself to be misled by what individual representatives of natural science would dictate to him. He must investigate in the spiritual as they do in the physical domain, but he need not adopt the opinions they entertain about the spiritual.

# II

## THE MYSTERIES AND THEIR WISDOM

A KIND of mysterious veil hangs over the manner in which spiritual needs were satisfied during the older civilisations by those who sought a deeper religious life and fuller knowledge than the popular religions offered. <note>In ancient times, "popular religions" refers to the state-sponsored worship of gods (like Zeus or Jupiter) which focused on public ritual, whereas the "Mysteries" were private, secret initiations for those seeking personal spiritual growth.</note> If we inquire how these needs were satisfied, we find ourselves led into the dim twilight of the **mysteries**, and the individual seeking them disappears for a time from our observation. We see how it is that the popular religions cannot give him what his heart desires. He acknowledges the existence of the gods, but knows that the ordinary ideas about them do not solve the great problems of existence. <note>The "great problems" usually refer to the origin of life, the nature of the human spirit, and what happens to the soul after death.</note> He seeks a **wisdom** which is jealously guarded by a community of **priest-sages**. <term>priest-sages: leadership figures in ancient societies who held both religious authority and deep philosophical or scientific knowledge.</term> His aspiring **soul** seeks a refuge in this community. If he is found by the sages

When he reappears in the light of day a different, quite transformed person is before us. We see a man who cannot find words sublime enough to express the momentous experience through which he has passed. Not merely metaphorically but in a most real sense does he seem to have gone through the **gate of death** and to have awakened to a new and higher life. <note>The 'gate of death' refers to a symbolic or psychological death of the former self, allowing the seeker to be 'reborn' with a higher spiritual consciousness.</note> He is, moreover, quite certain that no one who has not had a similar experience can understand his words.

The enigma becomes still more puzzling when we discover that the ancients looked upon the **Mysteries** as something dangerous. There was no greater crime than the "**betrayal**" of secrets to the **uninitiated**. The "traitor" was punished with death and the confiscation of his property. We know that the poet **Æschylus** <note>Æschylus (c. 525–456 BCE) was the earliest of the three great Greek tragedians. For others there is only misery and hardship."

Is one therefore not describing dangers when speaking of the **Mysteries**? Is it not robbing a man of happiness and of the best part of his life to take him to the portals of the **nether-world**? Terrible is the responsibility incurred by such an act. And yet ought we to refuse that responsibility? These were the questions which the **initiate** had to put to himself. He was of opinion that his knowledge bore the same relation to the soul of the people as light does to darkness. But innocent happiness dwells in that darkness, and the **Mystics** were of opinion that that happiness should not be sacrilegiously interfered with.

A divinity approaches you. It is either everything or nothing. Nothing, if you meet it in the frame of mind with which you confront everyday matters. Everything, if you are prepared, and attuned to the meeting. # Christianity as Mystical Fact

The Mystic called the power that flashed up within him a <term>daimon</term> <note>original: "daimon" (Greek δαίμων). In the ancient world, a daimon was not a "demon" in the modern sense, but a guiding spirit or a divine spark that exists between the human realm and the gods.</note>. He was himself the product of this <term>daimon</term>. It seemed to him as though another being had entered him and taken possession of his organs, a being standing between his physical personality and the all-ruling cosmic power, the divinity.

The Mystic sought this—his <term>daimon</term>. He said to himself: "I have become a human being in mighty Nature, but Nature did not complete her task. This completion I must take in hand myself. But I cannot accomplish it in the gross kingdom of nature to which my physical personality belongs. What it is possible to develop in that realm has already been developed. Therefore I must leave this kingdom and take up the building in the realm of the spirit at the point where nature left off. I must create an atmosphere of life not to be found in outer nature."

This atmosphere of life was prepared for the Mystic in the <term>Mystery temples</term> <note>Mystery temples: specialized ancient centers of initiation—such as those at Eleusis or in Egypt—where students were taught to awaken their spiritual faculties through rigorous training and ritual.</note>. There the forces slumbering within him were awakened, there he was changed into a higher creative spirit-nature. This transformation

Therefore initiates went back to beings of a higher nature than <term>daimons</term> or gods, and this was characteristic of the essence of the wisdom of the Mysteries.

### Christianity as Mystical Fact

The people represented the gods and <term>daimons</term> <note>daimons: in the Greek tradition, these are spiritual beings that act as intermediaries between the divine and the human world; they are often seen as guiding spirits or expressions of a higher power within the soul.</note> in images borrowed from the world of <term>sense-reality</term> <note>sense-reality: the physical world as perceived through the eyes, ears, and touch, which the author contrasts with the "Eternal" or spiritual world.</note>. Would not one who had penetrated into the nature of the Eternal doubt about the eternal nature of such gods as these? How could the <term>Zeus</term> <note>Zeus: the king of the Olympian gods, often depicted in mythology with human emotions and physical limitations.</note> of popular imagination be eternal if he bore within him the qualities of a perishable being?

One thing was clear to the <term>Mystics</term> <note>Mystics: here, those who sought a direct, internal experience of spiritual truth through the secret rites of the ancient Mysteries, rather than relying on external myths.</note>, that man arrives at a conception of the gods in a different way from the conception of other things. An object belonging to the outer world compels us to form a very definite idea of it. In contrast to this, we form our conception of the gods in a freer and somewhat arbitrary manner. The control of the outer world is absent. Reflection teaches us that what we conceive as gods is not subject to outer control. This places us in logical uncertainty; we begin to feel that we ourselves are the creators of our gods. Indeed, we ask ourselves how we have arrived at a conception of the universe that goes beyond <term>physical reality</term> <note>physical reality: the material world governed by natural laws; the text suggests the seeker must look beyond this to find the true source of spiritual concepts.</note>. The <term>initiate</term> <note>initiate: a student of the Mysteries who has been "awakened" to spiritual realities through specialized training and symbolic rituals.</note> was obliged to ask himself such questions; his doubts were justified. He has life, a perceptible life, wandering amongst men. He is the god freed from enchantment, the offspring of the God who was hidden by a spell. He is not the great God, who was and is and is to come, but yet he may be taken, in a certain sense, as the revelation of Him. It is the birth of the Divine. It is an event as real as any natural event, only enacted upon a higher plane.

# III

## THE GREEK SAGES BEFORE PLATO IN THE LIGHT OF THE WISDOM OF THE MYSTERIES

->39<-

Numerous facts combine to show us that the philosophical wisdom of the Greeks rested on the same mental basis as mystical knowledge. We only understand the great philosophers when we approach them with feelings gained through study of the **Mysteries** <term>Mysteries: sacred rites and secret teachings of ancient Greece (such as the Eleusinian or Orphic Mysteries) where participants were initiated into hidden knowledge about life, death, and the soul.</term>. Heraclitus speaks vehemently against Homer and Hesiod, and the learned men of his day. <note>Homer and Hesiod were the most famous Greek poets. He did not desire gods endowed with qualities taken from a perishable world, and he could not regard as a **supreme science** <term>supreme science: In this context, it refers to 'Gnosis' or spiritual wisdom—the understanding of the underlying principles of existence rather than just collecting facts about the physical world.</term> that science which investigates the growth and decay of things. Just as the tension in a bow or a lyre string creates power or music by pulling in opposite directions, the universe creates unity through the tension of opposing forces.</note> What depths are hidden in this image! ->47<-

Whether he meant "fire" as physical fire, or whether fire for him was only a symbol of eternal spirit which dissolves and reconstitutes all things, this is putting a wrong construction upon his thought. He meant both and neither of these things. For spirit was also alive, for him, in ordinary fire, and the force which is physically active in fire lives on a higher plane in the human soul, which melts in its crucible <note>A crucible is a ceramic or metal container in which metals or other substances may be melted or subjected to very high temperatures; here, it refers to the soul's power to transform raw experience.</note> mere sense-knowledge, so that out of this the contemplation of the eternal may arise.

It is very easy to misunderstand Heraclitus. He makes Strife the "Father of things," <note>original: "War is the father of all." Heraclitus argued that conflict between opposites is what creates the variety of the world.</note> but only of "things," not of the eternal. If there were no contradictions in the world, if the most multifarious interests were not opposing each other, the world of becoming, of transitory things, would not exist. <term>World of becoming: In philosophy, this refers to the physical world we perceive with our senses, which is constantly changing, growing, and decaying, as opposed to the "world of being" which is eternal.</term> But what is revealed in this antagonism, what is poured forth into it, is not strife but harmony. Just because there is strife in all things, the spirit of the wise should pass over them like a breath of fire, and change them into harmony.

great thoughts of Heraclitean wisdom. <note>Heraclitus was a pre-Socratic Greek philosopher (c. 535 – c. 475 BCE) famous for the idea that "everything flows" and that fire is the fundamental substance of the universe.</note> What is man as a personal being? From the above point of view Heraclitus is able to answer. Man is composed of the conflicting elements into which divinity has poured itself. In this state he finds himself, and beyond this becomes aware of the spirit within him,—the spirit which is rooted in the eternal. But the spirit itself is born, for man, out of the conflict of elements, and it is the first which has to calm them. In man, Nature surpasses her natural limits. It is indeed the same universal force which created antagonism and the mixture of elements which is afterwards, by its wisdom, to do away with the conflict. It is the peculiarity of the human soul that a temporal thing should be able to work like an eternal one, should grow and increase in power like an eternal thing. This is why the soul is at once like a god and a worm. <note>This vivid imagery illustrates the human paradox: containing the highest spiritual potential while being tied to the lowest physical existence.</note> Man, owing to this, stands in a mid-position between God and animals. He extends man's vital essence far beyond the personal. The personality is the vehicle of the **daimon**, which is not confined within the limit of the personality, and for which the birth and death of the personality are of no importance. What is the relation of the daimonic element to the personality which comes and goes? The personality is only a form for the manifestation of the daimon.

A different conception of the universe from that of Heraclitus grew up, on the basis of the Mysteries, in the community founded by Pythagoras <note>Pythagoras (c. 570–495 BCE) was a philosopher and mathematician who established a secretive, communal school in Croton, Italy, where he taught that the soul is immortal and the universe is governed by mathematical ratios.</note> in the 6th century B.C. in Southern Italy. The Pythagoreans saw the basis of things in the numbers and geometrical figures of which they investigated the laws by means of mathematics. Aristotle <note>Aristotle (384–322 BCE) was a student of Plato and one of the most influential figures in Western philosophy; his writings often summarize and critique the thinkers who came before him.</note> says of them: “They first studied mathematics, and, quite engrossed in them, they considered the elements of mathematics to be the elements of all things. Now as numbers are naturally the first thing in mathematics...”

Geometrical figures also play a similar rôle. <term>Astronomy</term><note>From the Greek 'astron' (star) and 'nomos' (law); literally, the laws of the stars.</note>, for instance, is mathematics applied to the heavenly bodies. One fact became important to the thought-life of the Pythagoreans. This was that man, quite alone and purely through his mental activity, discovers the laws of numbers and figures, and yet, that when he looks abroad into nature, he finds that things are obeying the same laws which he has ascertained for himself in his own mind. Man forms the idea of an <term>ellipse</term><note>A regular oval shape. What may be said about these may equally be said of <term>Copernican views</term><note>Referring to Nicolaus Copernicus’s 16th-century realization that the Earth revolves around the Sun, a shift in perspective that, like the Pythagorean view, relied on mathematical calculation over simple sensory observation.</note> in the connection now being dealt with.) Hence it follows as a direct consequence that the achievements of the human soul are not an activity apart from the rest of the world, but that in those achievements the cosmic laws are expressed.

From such a point of view the <term>Pythagoreans</term><note>Followers of Pythagoras who sought to purify the soul through the study of music, mathematics, and philosophy.</note> must have considered that only fools could imagine the <term>soul-force</term><note>The vital, spiritual energy that animates the human being and connects them to the divine.</note> to be exhausted with the personality.

Knowledge for them meant intercourse with the eternal. The more man brought the eternal element within him into existence, the greater must he neces-

->63<-

The importance of the <term>Mysteries</term><note>Secret religious rites in ancient Greece, such as the Eleusinian Mysteries, which promised initiates a more profound understanding of life, death, and the divine.</note> to the spiritual life of the Greeks may be realised from **Plato’s** conception of the universe. There is only one way of understanding him thoroughly. It is to place him in the light which streams forth from the Mysteries.

Plato’s later disciples, the <term>Neo-Platonists</term><note>Followers of a philosophical and spiritual movement starting in the 3rd century AD that expanded upon Plato's ideas, often emphasizing the mystical union with "the One."</note>, credit him with a <term>secret doctrine</term><note>Also called the "unwritten doctrines," these are believed to be the high-level oral teachings Plato shared only with his most advanced students, which were never fully recorded in his public dialogues.</note> which he imparted only to those who were worthy, and which he conveyed under the "seal of secrecy." His teaching was looked upon as mysterious in the same sense as the wisdom of the Mysteries. Even if the <term>seventh Platonic letter</term><note>An autobiographical letter attributed to Plato (though its authorship is debated) that discusses his political experiences and his belief that the ultimate truths of philosophy cannot be written down in books.</note> is not from his hand, as is alleged, it does not signify for our present purpose, for it does not matter whether it was he or another who gave utterance to the...

The dying Socrates does not prove the immortality of the soul, he simply lays bare the nature of the soul. And then it comes to light that growth and decay, birth and death, have nothing to do with the soul. The essence of the soul lies in the true, and this can neither come into being nor perish. The soul has no more to do with the **becoming** <note>In Platonic philosophy, "becoming" (original Greek: genesis) refers to the physical world of change, growth, and decay, which is contrasted with the eternal world of "being."</note> than the straight has with the crooked. But death belongs to the becoming. Therefore the soul has nothing to do with death. Must we not say of what is **immortal**, that it admits of mortality as little as does the straight of the crooked? Starting from this point, "must we not ask," adds Socrates, "that if the immortal is imperishable, is it not impossible for the soul to come to an end when death arrives? For from what has been already shown, it does not admit of death, nor can it die any more than **three can be an even number**." <note>Socrates uses mathematical analogies to show that certain essences are fundamentally incompatible with certain qualities; just as the "oddness" of the number three cannot admit "evenness," the soul, which brings life, cannot admit death.</note>

Let us review the whole development of this dialogue, in which Socrates brings his

<summary>This page explores Socrates' argument in the Phaedo that the soul is inherently immortal because its essence belongs to the eternal realm of truth rather than the world of "becoming" and decay. Socrates posits that the soul is as incompatible with death as an odd number is with being even.</summary>

The listeners accept his thoughts, and they look into themselves to see if they can find in their inner experiences something which assents to his ideas. They raise the objections that occur to them. What has happened to the listeners when the dialogue is finished? They have discovered something within themselves that they did not previously possess. They have not merely accepted an abstract truth, but they have undergone a personal development. Something has come to life in them which was not living in them before. Is not this to be compared with an **initiation**? <note>In the ancient world, an initiation (or "telete") was a ritual process through which a person was introduced to sacred knowledge and "reborn" into a higher state of awareness.</note> And does not this throw light on the reason for Plato's setting forth his philosophy in the form of conversation? These dialogues are essentially the literary expression of the events that occurred within the sanctuaries of the **Mysteries**. <note>The Mysteries were secret religious cults of ancient Greece, such as the Eleusinian Mysteries, which used dramatic performances and rituals to teach truths about life, death, and the soul.</note> We are convinced of this from what Plato himself says in many passages. Plato desired, as a philosophical teacher, to be what the initiator into the Mysteries was, insofar as this was compatible with the philosophical manner of communication. It is evident how Plato feels himself in harmony

And Plato promises those who follow this path, that divinity, as a deliverer, will grant them illuminating teaching as the conclusion of their devious and wandering researches.

> “There have already been many and various destructions of part of the human race,” says the Egyptian priest to Solon, “and there will be more in the future; the most extensive by fire and water, other lesser ones through countless other causes. It is also related in your country that Phaëthon <note>Phaëthon: In Greek mythology, the son of the sun god who attempted to drive his father's solar chariot but lost control, nearly destroying the world with fire.</note>, the son of Helios <note>Helios: The personification of the Sun in ancient Greek religion and myth.</note>, once mounted his father’s chariot, and as he did not know how to drive it, everything on the earth was burnt up, and he himself slain by lightning. This sounds like a fable, but it contains the truth of the change in the movements of the celestial bodies revolving round the earth and of the annihilation of everything on the earth by much fire. This annihilation happens periodically, after the lapse of certain long periods of time.”

This passage in the *Timæus* contains a plain indication of the attitude of the **initiate** <note>Initiate: One who has been formally admitted into the secret knowledge and rites of a spiritual or religious tradition.</note> towards folk-myths. He recognises the truths hidden in their images.

In the *Symposium* <note>One of Plato's most famous dialogues, depicting a banquet where various guests give speeches in praise of Eros, the god of love.</note>, people of the most various ranks and views of life speak about love,—the ordinary man, the politician, the scientific man, the satiric poet **Aristophanes**, and the tragic poet **Agathon**. Thus love has something divine in it, and owing to this, each individual can only understand it as far as he participates in the divine.

For no wise woman on the physical plane could awaken the daimon in the soul, unless the daimonic force were latent in the soul itself. It is surely in **Socrates’** own soul that we must also look for this “wise woman.”

But there must be a reason why that which brings the <term>daimon</term> <note>original: "daimon"; an intermediate spirit or inner guiding voice connecting the human soul to the divine.</note> to life within the soul should appear as an outward being on the physical plane. Unconsciously it leads man to the divine, with the inevitableness of a natural force.

Hera, the mother of the gods, incites the <term>Titans</term> <note>In Greek mythology, the Titans were powerful deities who preceded the Olympian gods; here they represent the forces that fragment the unified spirit.</note> against Dionysos, and they tear him in pieces. But Pallas Athene rescues his heart, which is still beating, and brings it to Zeus. Out of it he engenders his son for the second time.

In this myth we can accurately trace a process which is enacted in the depths of the human soul. Interpreting it in the manner of the Egyptian priest who instructed **Solon** <note>Solon was a famous Athenian lawmaker; the story refers to his visit to Egypt where he was told that Greek myths were actually veiled versions of deep scientific and spiritual truths.</note> about the nature of myths (*cf. p. 78 et seq.*), we might say, it is related that Dionysos was the son of a god and a mortal mother, that he was torn in pieces and afterwards

# Christianity as Mystical Fact

In this <term>spiritual reality</term> <note>The realm of existence beyond the physical, where cosmic forces operate directly on the human being.</note> the soul is not merely experiencing something in itself, but it has been released from itself and is taking part in a <term>cosmic event</term> <note>An occurrence that affects the universal order, rather than just an individual’s internal psychology.</note>, which is not enacted within the soul, in reality, but outside it.

Platonic wisdom and Greek myths are closely linked together, so too are the myths and the wisdom of the <term>Mysteries</term> <note>Secret religious schools of antiquity (such as the Eleusinian or Orphic Mysteries) where initiates were taught hidden truths about life, death, and the divine.</note>. The created gods were the object of popular religion, while the history of their origin was the secret of the Mysteries. No wonder that it was held to be dangerous to "betray" the Mysteries, for thereby the origin of the gods of the people was "betrayed." And a right understanding of that origin is <term>salutary</term> <note>Beneficial or promoting spiritual health.</note>, while a misunderstanding is injurious.

## V

# THE WISDOM OF THE MYSTERIES AND THE MYTH

THE <term>Mystic</term> <note>A seeker who pursues direct, personal experience of divine truths or spiritual realities through inner contemplation.</note> sought forces and beings within himself which are unknown to man as long as he remains in the ordinary attitude towards life. The Mystic puts the great question about his own <term>spiritual forces</term> <note>The non-physical capacities of the human soul, such as intuition and higher perception, that go beyond basic intellect.</note> and the laws which transcend the <term>lower nature</term> <note>The part of human existence governed by physical instincts, sensory perceptions, and purely material needs.</note>. A man of ordinary views of life, bounded by the senses and logic, creates gods for himself, or when he gets to the point of seeing that he has made them, he disclaims them. The Mystic knows that he creates gods, he knows why he creates them, he sees, so to say, behind the <term>natural law</term> <note>The underlying principles of development that govern both the physical world and the evolution of human consciousness.</note> which makes man create them. It is as though a plant suddenly became conscious, and learned the laws of its growth and development. As it is, it develops in lovely uncon- <note>The text is interrupted here at the page break; the word is likely "unconsciousness."</note>

It is thus with the <term>Mystic</term> <note>A person who seeks direct, personal experience of divine or spiritual reality through inner contemplation.</note> with regard to the laws, the forces working within him. As one who knew, he was forced to create something divine beyond himself. And the <term>initiates</term> <note>Those who have been admitted into the secret knowledge and rituals of the ancient "Mystery schools."</note> took up the same attitude to that which the people had created beyond nature; that is to the world of popular gods and myths. They wanted to penetrate the laws of this world of gods and myths. Where the people saw the form of a god, or a myth, they looked for a higher truth.

Let us take an example. He was aware of being above that common consciousness, just as a botanist stands "above" or possesses a higher perspective of a growing plant. Something was expressed which was different from what was present in the literal, mythical consciousness, but it was looked upon as a deeper truth, symbolically expressed in the myth.

## Mysteries and the Myth

-> 7 <-

...the **initiate** <note>A person who has been "initiated" or introduced into secret spiritual knowledge or rituals not known to the general public.</note> knows that mystery. It points toward a power within the human personality that is unknown to ordinary, everyday awareness, yet is active nonetheless within it. This is the very force that generates the **myth**, which shares the same internal structure as mystical truth. In this way, the myth serves as a symbol for this truth.

What, then, can be found within myths? They contain a creation of the spirit—of the soul in its state of unconscious creativity. The soul operates according to specific, well-defined laws. To create something that transcends its own limitations, it must work in a specific direction. During the <term>mythological stage</term> <note>A phase of human development or consciousness where truth is expressed through stories and symbols rather than abstract logic.</note>, the soul performs this work through images; however, these images are built up according to the soul’s own inherent laws. We might also say that when the soul progresses beyond this stage of mythological consciousness toward deeper truths, these newer truths bear the same "stamp" as the myths did. This is because the exact same force was at work in the formation of both.

**Plotinus** <note>A major Greek-speaking philosopher (204–269 AD) and the founder of Neoplatonism, a philosophy that views the world as a series of emanations from a single, divine source.</note>, the philosopher of the **Neo-Platonic school**, speaks of this relationship between mythical representation and higher knowledge when referring to the priest-sages of Egypt <note>Ancient Egyptian priests were often regarded by later Greeks and Romans as the original keepers of deep, hidden scientific and spiritual wisdom.</note>. A man very much attached to life, who seeks sensuous pleasures and will die at no price is pursued by four serpents. He hears a voice commanding him to feed and bathe the serpents from time to time. The man runs away, fearing the serpents. Again he hears a voice, warning him that he is pursued by five murderers. Once more he escapes. A voice calls his attention to a sixth murderer, who is about to behead him with a sword. Again he flees. He comes to a deserted village. There he hears a voice telling him that robbers are shortly going to plunder the village. Having again escaped, he comes to a great flood. He feels unsafe where he is, and out of straw, wood, and leaves he makes a basket in which he arrives at the other shore. Now he is safe, he is a Brahmin <note>In this context, a **Brahmin** refers to someone who has reached a state of spiritual realization or holiness.</note>.

> They remain ever the same, but yet by combining their forces
> Become transformed into men and the numberless beings besides.
> These are now joined into one, love binding the many together,
> Now once again they are scattered, dispersing through hatred and strife.

What then are the things in the world from Empedocles' point of view? They are the elements in different combinations. They could only come into being because the **Primeval Unity** <term>Primeval Unity|In Greek philosophy, specifically that of Empedocles, this refers to the 'Sphairos,' a state where all elements are perfectly unified by the force of Love before being separated by Strife.</term> was broken up into the four essences. Therefore this primordial unity was poured into the elements. Anything confronting us is part of the divinity which was poured out. But the divinity is hidden in the thing; it first had to die that things might come into being. And what are these things? Mixtures of divine constituents effectuated by love and hatred. Hephaistos, the son of Zeus, was commissioned to make a female form of great beauty, whom the gods adorned with every possible gift. She was called **Pandora**, the all-gifted one. <note>original: "Pandora," derived from the Greek *pan* (all) and *doron* (gift).</note> Hermes, messenger of the gods, brought her to Epimetheus, the brother of Prometheus. She brought him a casket, as a present from the gods. Epimetheus accepted the present, although Prometheus had warned him against receiving any gift from the gods. 

When the casket was opened, every possible human evil flew out of it. **Hope** alone remained, and this because Pandora quickly closed the box. Hope has therefore been left to man, as a doubtful gift of the gods. <note>The author suggests hope is "doubtful" because it can be a form of illusion or a distraction from spiritual reality, even as it provides comfort.</note>

## Christianity as Mystical Fact

But he is not altogether a **Titan**. Prometheus is thus the representative of those worlds which have given man the progressive element—half nature-force, half spiritual force—**man's will**. 

The will points on the one side towards good, and on the other, towards evil. Its fate is decided according as it leans to the spiritual or the perishable. This fate is that of man himself. He is chained to the perishable, the eagle gnaws him, and he has to suffer. <note>The eagle, which in the myth devours Prometheus's liver, is interpreted here as the consuming nature of lower desires and earthly cares that "eat away" at the human soul when it is bound solely to the physical world.</note> He can only reach the highest by seeking his destiny in solitude.

The **centaur** <note>A mythological creature with the upper body of a human and the lower body of a horse, representing the duality of human nature: the aspiring spirit and the grounded animal instincts.</note> is man himself, half animal, half spiritual. He must die in order that the purely spiritual man may be delivered. That which is disdained by **Prometheus**, human will, is accepted by **Epimetheus**, reason or prudence. <note>In Greek, Prometheus means "foresight," while his brother Epimetheus means "afterthought." The thread running through the legends of the **Argonauts**, **Heracles** and **Prometheus**, is continued in **Homer's** *Odyssey*. Here we find ourselves compelled to use our own method of interpretation. ### Mysteries and the Myth

vol. iii, part 3.) The wisdom that qualified one for the priesthood was the wisdom of the Greek Mysteries. The festivals, which were celebrated twice a year, represented the great world-drama of the destiny of the divine within the world and the destiny of the human soul. The Lesser Mysteries took place in February, and the Greater Mysteries in September. Initiations were connected with these festivals. The symbolical presentation of the cosmic and human drama formed the final act of the initiations of the Mystics <note>The *mystae*, or candidates for initiation, who sought to understand the hidden truths of existence.</note>, which took place here.

The Eleusinian temples had been erected in honour of the goddess Demeter <note>The goddess of the harvest and agriculture, who presided over grains and the fertility of the earth.</note>. She was a daughter of Kronos <note>In Greek mythology, the Titan who ruled during the Golden Age.</note>. She had given to Zeus a daughter, Persephone, before his marriage with Hera. Persephone, while playing, was carried away by Hades (Pluto), the god of the infernal regions <note>The underworld, representing the realm of the dead or the unconscious.</note>. Demeter wandered far and wide over the earth, seeking her with lamentations. Sitting on a stone in Eleusis <note>Later known as the "Laughless Stone," where the goddess rested in her grief.</note>, she was found by the daughters of Keleus, ruler of the place; in the form of an old woman she entered the service of his family, as nurse to the queen’s son. Karl Richard Lepsius was a pioneering Prussian Egyptologist who published the first modern edition of this text.</note>). Horus takes up the earthly tasks of Osiris. He is the second Osiris, still imperfect, but progressing towards the true Osiris.

The true Osiris is in the human soul, which at first is of a transitory nature; but as such, it is destined to give birth to the eternal. Man may, therefore, regard himself as the tomb of Osiris. The lower nature (Typhon <note>The Greek name for the Egyptian god Set, the deity of chaos and storms who represents the destructive forces within the self.</note>) has killed the higher nature in him. Love in his soul (Isis <note>The goddess who represents the nurturing, protective aspect of the soul that preserves spiritual potential.</note>) must take care of the dead fragments of his body, and then the higher nature, the eternal soul (Horus <note>The son of Osiris and Isis, representing the reborn and developing spiritual consciousness.</note>) will be born, which can progress to Osiris life.

## VII

### THE GOSPELS

The accounts of the life of Jesus which can be submitted to historical examination are contained in the **Gospels** <term>Gospels: The first four books of the New Testament (Matthew, Mark, Luke, and John) that provide the primary narrative of Jesus's life and ministry.</term>. All that does not come from this source might—in the opinion of one of those who are considered the greatest historical authorities on the subject, **Adolf von Harnack** <note>Adolf von Harnack (1851–1930) was a leading German theologian and historian who pioneered the historical-critical method of studying early Christian texts.</note>—be "easily written on a quarto page" <note>A quarto page is a book size created by folding a large sheet of paper twice to form four leaves; Harnack uses this term to emphasize how little information about Jesus exists outside the Gospels.</note>.

But what kind of documents are these Gospels? The fourth, that of **St. John**, differs so much from the others, that those who think themselves obliged to follow the path of historical research in order to study the subject, come to the conclusion: "If John possesses the genuine tradition about the life of Jesus, that of the first three Evangelists (the **Synoptists** <term>Synoptists: The authors of the Gospels of Matthew, Mark, and Luke, so called because their accounts are so similar they can be "viewed together" (from the Greek 'synopsis').</term>) is untenable. If the Synoptists are right, the Fourth Gospel must be

There are various elements in the **Gospels**. Firstly, facts are related, which seem to lay claim to being historical. Secondly, there are **parables**, in which the narrative form is only used to symbolise a deeper truth. And, thirdly, there are teachings characteristic of the Christian conception of life. In **St. John’s Gospel** there is no real parable. The source from which he drew was a **mystical school** which considered parables unnecessary.

The part played by ostensibly historical facts and parables in the first three Gospels is clearly shown in the narrative of the **cursing of the fig tree**. ## VIII

### THE RAISING OF LAZARUS <note>The text refers to this as "The Lazarus Miracle." In the context of mystical Christianity, this "miracle" is often interpreted as a public performance of an ancient initiation rite.</note>

Amongst the “miracles” attributed to **Jesus**, very special importance must be attached to the raising of **Lazarus** at **Bethany**. Everything combines to assign a prominent position in the **New Testament** to that which is here related by the **Evangelist**. We must bear in mind that **St. John** alone relates it, the **Evangelist** who by the weighty words with which he opens his **Gospel** claims for it a very definite interpretation.

**St. John** begins with these sentences: “**In the beginning was the Word, and the Word was with God, and the word was a God.** <note>original: "und ein Gott war das Wort." This specific phrasing—"a God"—is a translation of the Greek "theos en ho logos," distinguishing the "Word" as a divine being without identifying it identically with the Father (the absolute Godhead).</note> . . . **And the Word was made flesh, and dwelt among us, and we beheld his glory, a glory as of the only begotten of the Father, full of grace and truth.**” <note>A reference to John 1:1 and 1:14. It would be a triviality to think that Jesus meant to say that Lazarus had only become ill in order that Jesus might manifest His skill through him. And it would again be a triviality to think that Jesus meant to assert that faith in Him brings to life again one who in the ordinary sense is dead. <note>The author uses the word "triviality" to suggest that a purely literal or physical miracle would be beneath the spiritual dignity of Jesus's mission.</note>

What would there be remarkable about a person who has risen from the dead, if after his resurrection he were the same as he was before dying? # Christianity as Mystical Fact

New life comes forth from death. This life has outlived death. Man has gained confidence in the new life.

It happened thus with Lazarus. Jesus had prepared him for resurrection. His illness was at once symbolic and real, an illness which was an <term>initiation</term> <note>Initiation: A ritualized process of spiritual transformation where an individual is guided through a symbolic death to "awaken" with higher wisdom.</note> (*cf. p. 132 et seq.*), and which leads, after three days, to a really new life.

Lazarus was ripe for undergoing this experience. He wrapped himself in the garment of the **Mystic**, and fell into a condition of lifelessness which was symbolic death. And when Jesus came, the three days had elapsed. <note>The "three days" refers to the traditional period in ancient Mystery schools during which the candidate remained in a death-like trance while their soul underwent spiritual trials.</note>

> "Then they took away the stone from the place where the dead was laid. And Jesus lifted up his eyes and said, 'Father, I thank thee that thou hast heard me'" (John xi. 41).

An initiation into the <term>Mysteries</term> <note>Mysteries: Secret religious and philosophical rites of the ancient world (such as those in Egypt or Greece) where sacred truths about life, death, and the soul were revealed to the "initiated."</note> had been consummated. In Lazarus, Jesus accomplished the great miracle of the <term>transmutation of life</term> <note>The transformation of the soul's nature, moving from a mere physical existence to a higher spiritual awakening.</note> in the sense of immemorial tradition. Through this event, Christianity is connected with the <term>Mysteries</term> <note>The "Mysteries" refer to the sacred, secret traditions of antiquity (like those in Egypt or Greece) where individuals were initiated into the secrets of the soul and the cosmos.</note>. Lazarus had become an <term>initiate</term> <note>A person who has undergone a spiritual ritual to gain direct knowledge of higher worlds.</note> through Christ Jesus Himself, and had thereby become able to enter the higher worlds. He was at once the first Christian initiate and the first to be initiated by Christ Jesus Himself. 

Through his initiation, he had become capable of recognizing that the "Word" <note>original: "Word" (Logos). In mystical traditions, the "Word" is the divine creative power that speaks the universe into being.</note> which had been awakened within him had become a person in Christ Jesus, and that consequently there stood before him in the personality of his awakener, the same force which had been spiritually manifested within him. From this point of view, these words of Jesus are significant:

> "And I knew that thou hearest me always: but because of the people which stand by I said it, that they may believe that thou hast sent me."

In the words, “Lazarus, come forth,” we can recognise the call with which the **Egyptian priestly initiators** summoned back to every-day life those who, temporarily removed from the world by the processes of initiation, had undergone them in order to die to earthly things and to gain a conviction of the reality of the eternal. Jesus in this way revealed the secret of the Mysteries. It is easy to understand that the Jews could not let such an act go unpunished, any more than the Greeks could have refrained from punishing <term>Æschylus</term> <note>Aeschylus (c. 525–456 BC), the famous Greek dramatist, was reportedly prosecuted for "profaning" the Eleusinian Mysteries by depicting certain secret symbols or themes in his plays.</note>, if he had betrayed the secrets of the Mysteries.

The main point for Jesus was to represent in the initiation of Lazarus before all “the people which stood by,” an event which in the old days of priestly wisdom could only be enacted in the recesses of the mystery-temples. The initiation of Lazarus was to prepare the way to the understanding of the “**Mystery of Golgotha**” <note>This term, frequently used in Christian esotericism, refers to the spiritual significance of the Crucifixion and Resurrection of Jesus as a transformative event for all of humanity, not just for secret initiates.</note>. Previously only those who “saw,” that is to say, who were initiated, were able to know something of

# IX

## THE APOCALYPSE OF ST. JOHN

At the end of the New Testament stands a remarkable document, the <term>Apocalypse</term> <note>From the Greek 'apokalypsis,' meaning an 'unveiling' or 'revelation' of hidden divine secrets.</note>, the secret Revelation of St. John. We have only to read the opening words to feel the deep mystic character of this book.

> "The Revelation of Jesus Christ, which God gave unto him, to shew unto his servants how the necessary things are shortly going to happen; and this is sent in signs by the angel of God unto his servant John." <note>This is a translation of Revelation 1:1. The phrase "sent in signs" is a specific interpretation of the Greek word 'esēmanen,' which suggests the content is communicated through symbols rather than literal descriptions.</note>

What is here revealed is "sent in signs." Therefore we must not take the literal meaning of the words as they stand, but seek for a deeper meaning of which the words are only signs. But there are other things also which point to a hidden meaning. St. John addresses himself to the seven churches in Asia. Not actual, material churches are meant; the number seven is <note>In mystical traditions, the number seven often represents a complete cycle or a totality; here, the "seven churches" are frequently interpreted as representing seven stages of human spiritual development or different types of spiritual communities.</note>

<summary>This page introduces the Apocalypse of St. John as a mystical text that must be interpreted through its symbols—or "signs"—rather than literally. It begins to explore the symbolic significance of the "seven churches in Asia" mentioned in the opening of the Book of Revelation.</summary>

<keywords>Apocalypse, St. John, Revelation, signs, symbolism, seven churches, Asia, New Testament, mysticism, esoteric interpretation</keywords>

The actual number of the <term>Asiatic churches</term> <note>While historical records identify several early Christian communities in Asia Minor, the author suggests the choice of exactly seven is a deliberate spiritual choice rather than a literal census.</note> was different. And the manner in which <term>St. John</term> <note>Traditionally identified as the Apostle John, the author of the fourth Gospel and three Epistles.</note> arrived at the revelation also points to something mysterious. “I was in the Spirit on the Lord’s day, and heard behind me a great voice, as of a trumpet, saying, ‘What thou seest, write in a book, and send it unto the seven churches.’” Thus, we have to do with a revelation received by St. John in the spirit. And it is the revelation of Jesus Christ. Wrapped in a hidden meaning there appears what Christ Jesus manifested to the world. Therefore we must also look for this hidden meaning in the teachings of Christ. This revelation bears the same relation to ordinary Christianity as was borne by the revelation of the <term>Mysteries</term> <note>The ancient "Mysteries" were secret religious rites (like those at Eleusis) where initiates were taught deeper spiritual truths hidden from the general public.</note>, in pre-Christian times, to the people’s religion. On this account the attempt to treat the <term>Apocalypse</term> <note>From the Greek 'apokalypsis,' meaning an 'unveiling' or 'revelation' of hidden divine secrets.</note> as a mystery appears to be justified.

He has given a direction to the great creative thoughts which, through them, leads to wisdom. At the opening of the **first seal**, St. John sees a **white horse**, on which sits a rider with a bow. The first universal power, an embodiment of **Creative Thought**, becomes visible. It is put into the right direction by the new rider, **Christianity**. Strife is allayed by the new faith. At the opening of the **second seal** a **red horse** appears, ridden by one who takes away from the earth Peace—the second universal power—so that humanity may not neglect, through sloth, to cultivate divine things. The opening of the **third seal** shows the universal power of **Justice**, guided by Christianity. The fourth brings the power of **Religion** which, through Christianity, has received new dignity.

The meaning of the **four beasts** thus becomes plain. They are the four chief universal powers, to which Christianity gives a new direction: **War** (the lion); **Peaceful Work**...

# X

## JESUS AND HIS HISTORICAL BACKGROUND

IN the wisdom of the **Mysteries** <note>The secret religious schools of the ancient world that taught spiritual truths through symbolic rituals.</note> is to be sought the soil out of which grew the spirit of **Christianity**. All that was needed was the gaining ground of the fundamental conviction that this spirit must be introduced into life in greater measure than had been the case with the Mysteries. But such a conviction was widely spread, as may be seen from the manner of life of the **Essenes** <term>Essenes</term> <note>A Jewish sect known for communal living, ritual purity, and intense spiritual discipline.</note> and **Therapeutæ** <term>Therapeutæ</term> <note>A group of Jewish ascetics in Egypt related to the Essenes, whose name implies they were "healers" of the soul.</note>, who existed long before Christianity arose.

The **Essenes** were a secluded sect, living in **Palestine** <note>The historical region where the events of the New Testament took place.</note>, whose numbers at the time of Christ were estimated at four thousand. They formed a community which required that its members should lead a life which developed a higher life within the **soul**, and brought about a **new birth** <note>A transformation of the inner self, moving from mundane concerns to a higher spiritual awareness.</note>. The aspirant

<summary>This page describes the Essenes and Therapeutæ as precursors to Christianity, explaining how these groups sought to bring the secret wisdom of the Mysteries into daily life.</summary>
<keywords>Mysteries, Christianity, Essenes, Therapeutæ, Palestine, soul, new birth, historical background</keywords>

--- Page 217 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of the Essenes' initiation process and introduces the Therapeutæ of Egypt as a related group, citing the philosopher Philo and modern scholarship by G. R. Mead to establish the historical environment of early Christianity.</meta>

for admission was subjected to a severe test, in order to ascertain whether he were ripe for preparing himself for a higher life. If he was admitted, he had to undergo a period of probation, and to take a solemn oath that he would not betray to strangers the secrets of the **Essenian discipline** <term>Essenian discipline</term> <note>The rigorous code of conduct and spiritual training practiced by the Essenes, focusing on moral purity and communal devotion.</note>. The object of this life was the conquest of the lower nature in man, so that the **spirit latent within him** <note>The concept of an "inner spirit" refers to a divine or higher consciousness that remains dormant until awakened through ethical living and meditation.</note> might be awakened ever more and more. One who had experienced up to a certain point the spirit within him was raised to a higher grade, and enjoyed a corresponding degree of authority, not forced from without, but conditioned by the nature of things.

Akin to the **Essenes** were the **Therapeutæ** <term>Therapeutæ</term> <note>A community of Jewish ascetics in Egypt; their name is Greek for "healers" or "servants," signifying their goal of healing the soul from the "diseases" of worldly passions.</note>, who dwelt in **Egypt**. We get all desirable details of their mode of life in a treatise by the philosopher **Philo** <note>Philo of Alexandria (c. 20 BCE – 50 CE) was a Hellenistic Jewish thinker who attempted to bridge the gap between Hebrew scripture and Greek philosophy.</note>, *On the Contemplative Life* <note>original title: *De Vita Contemplativa*</note>. (The dispute as to the authenticity of this work must now be regarded as settled, and it may be rightly assumed that Philo really described the life of a community existing long before Christianity, and well known to him. **Compare** <note>original: "Cf.", an abbreviation for the Latin *confer*.</note> on the subject, G. R. Mead's *Fragments of a Faith Forgotten* <note>George Robert Stow Mead (1863–1933) was a prominent scholar who explored the gnostic and esoteric origins of Christianity.</note>.)

> “The dwellings of the members of the community are extremely simple, only affording necessary shelter from extreme heat and cold. The dwellings are not built close together, as in towns, for contiguity has no attraction for one who wishes for solitude; nor are they at a great distance one from another, in order that the social relations, so dear to them, may not be made difficult, and that they may easily be able to assist each other in case of an attack by brigands. In each house is a consecrated room called a temple or *<term>monasterion</term>* <note>original: "μοναστήριον"; here meaning a "sanctuary" or "closet" for solitary prayer, from which the modern word "monastery" is derived.</note>, a small room or cell in which the mysteries of the higher life are cultivated. . . . Their interpretation of sacred writings is directed to the deeper meaning of allegorical narratives.”

We thus see that what had been striven after in the narrower circle of the **Mysteries** <note>The "Mysteries" refer to the private religious rites of the ancient world, where secret knowledge about the soul and the divine was revealed to initiated members.</note> was being made general. The **Essene** <term>Essene</term> <note>A Jewish mystical sect known for communal living and strict adherence to purity, active in Palestine during the Second Temple period.</note> and **Therapeutic** <term>Therapeutic</term> <note>Referring to the Therapeutæ, the Jewish ascetic group in Egypt described by Philo as "healers" of souls.</note> communities form a natural transition from the **Mysteries** <note>The "Mysteries" refer to the private, secret religious rites of the ancient world where initiates were taught spiritual truths through direct experience.</note> to **Christianity**. But Christianity wished to extend to humanity in general what with the Essenes and Therapeutæ was an affair of a sect. This of course prepared the way for a still further weakening of the old severe forms.

The existence of such sects makes it possible to understand how far the time was ripe for the comprehension of the **mystery of Christ**. In the Mysteries, a man was artificially prepared for the dawning upon his consciousness, at the appropriate time, of the spiritual world. Within the Essene or Therapeutic community the soul sought, by a certain mode of life, to become ripe for the awakening of the higher man. A further step forward is that man struggles through to a feeling that a human individuality may have evolved to higher and higher stages of perfection in **repeated earth lives** <note>This refers to the concept of reincarnation, or the soul's journey through many successive lifetimes to reach spiritual maturity.</note>. One who had arrived at a glimpse of this truth would also be able to feel that in **Jesus** a being of lofty spirituality had appeared. The loftier the

A core concept in "negative theology" which suggests that God is not just a "thing" that exists, but is a reality that transcends the very categories of existence and non-existence.</note>. Thus the Church is through Jesus united to God: Jesus is its meaning and crowning-point.

### Christianity as Mystical Fact

One thing was clear to all **Gnosis** <term>Gnosis: From the Greek for "knowledge." The Christian had to do with the latter, and also with a perfect God, far above all that is attainable by humanity. If we hold firmly to this point of view, a fundamental mystic attitude of the soul is only possible when the soul's spiritual eyes are opened; when, through finding higher spiritual possibilities within itself, the soul throws itself open to the light which issues from **Christ** in **Jesus** <note>The author makes a distinction between "Christ"—the cosmic, divine principle—and "Jesus"—the historical human figure. Christianity is presented here as the event where these two became one.</note>. The union of the soul with its highest powers is at the same time union with the **historical Christ**. For mysticism is an immediate consciousness and feeling of the divine within the soul. But a God far transcending everything human can never dwell in the soul in the real sense of the word.
