<meta>This page continues the discussion on the tension between internal mystical experience and the external, objective God of Christianity. It picks up from the previous page's conclusion that a God who completely transcends human experience creates a gap that the mystic seeks to bridge.</meta>

# The Nature of Christianity

Christian mysticism <term>Christian mysticism: The practice of seeking a direct, personal experience of or union with the Divine, often through prayer, meditation, or contemplation, rather than through doctrine alone.</term> represents the effort, in some way or other, to lay hold of that God, and to apprehend Him directly in the soul. 

A conflict in this case was inevitable. It was really only possible for a man to find his own divine part, but this is both human and divine—the divine at a certain stage of development. Yet the Christian God is a definite one, perfect in himself. It was possible for a person to find in himself the power to strive upwards to this God, but he could not say that what he experienced in his own soul, at any stage of development, was one with God. A great gulf was fixed between what it was possible to find in the soul, and what Christianity called divine. It is the gulf between science <note>The author uses "science" here in the sense of *Wissenschaft*—a systematic, structured pursuit of knowledge or "knowing"—contrasted against "faith" which relies on belief in things unseen.</note> and faith, between knowledge and religious feeling. 

This gulf does not exist for the **Mystic** in the old sense of the word <note>The "old sense" refers to the traditions of the ancient Mystery schools (like those of Egypt or Greece), where the seeker believed they could actually become divine through a process of initiation.</note>. For he knows for a certainty that he can only comprehend the divine by degrees, and he also knows why this is so. It is clear to him that this gradual attainment is a real attainment of real divine life, and he finds it difficult to speak of a perfect, isolated divine principle.

<summary>This page explores the fundamental tension in Christian mysticism: the "gulf" between an individual's internal spiritual growth and the external, perfect God of the Church. It contrasts the Christian experience of faith with the ancient mystic's path of gradual, direct knowledge.</summary>

<keywords>Christian mysticism, the soul, nature of God, science vs faith, spiritual knowledge, religious feeling, ancient mysteries, divine development</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the exploration of the distinction between the "old" mystic, who seeks to bridge the gap between human and divine through stages of knowledge, and the specifically Christian mystic who relies on the historical figure of Christ.</meta>

# Christianity as Mystical Fact

A Mystic of this kind does not seek a perfect God, but he wishes to experience the divine life. He seeks to be made divine <note>This refers to the concept of "theosis" or deification, an ancient Christian idea that the purpose of human life is to become united with God’s nature.</note>, not to gain an external relation to the Godhead <term>Godhead: The essential nature or "being" of God, often distinguished from the personal character of God the Father, Son, or Spirit.</term>.

It is of the essence of Christianity that its mysticism in this sense starts with an assumption. The Christian Mystic seeks to behold divinity within him, but at the same time he looks up to the historical Christ as his physical eyes do to the sun. Just as the sun is the means by which physical eyes behold physical objects, so does the Christian Mystic intensify his inner nature that it may behold the divine, and the light which makes such vision possible for him is the fact of the appearance of Christ. It is He who enables man to attain his highest possibilities. It is in this way that the Christian Mystics of the Middle Ages differ from the Mystics of the ancient Mysteries <note>The "ancient Mysteries" refers to secret religious rites in ancient Greece, Egypt, and Rome (like the Eleusinian Mysteries), where participants were initiated into hidden spiritual truths.</note> (cf. my book, *Mystics of the Renaissance*).

<summary>This page defines Christian mysticism as a process of internal deification made possible by the historical appearance of Christ, who acts as the spiritual "sun" or light for the soul. It distinguishes this specifically Christian path from the older traditions of the ancient Mystery schools.</summary>

<keywords>Christian mysticism, theosis, Godhead, Christology, historical Christ, Middle Ages, ancient Mysteries, spiritual vision</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the general definition of Christian mysticism—defined by the historical presence of Christ—to the specific historical context of the first century. # XII Christianity and Heathen Wisdom

At the time of the first beginnings of Christianity, there appear in non-Christian <note>original: "heathen"</note> civilization certain conceptions of the universe which seem to be a continuation of Platonic philosophy. These views may also be understood as a deepening and spiritualization of the wisdom found in the ancient Mysteries <note>The "Mysteries" were secret religious traditions of the ancient world (such as those at Eleusis or Egypt) where participants were initiated into hidden spiritual truths through ritual and symbolic drama.</note>.

The beginning of such conceptions is dated to Philo of Alexandria (approx. 25 B.C.—50 A.D.). From his point of view, the processes that lead toward the divine take place in the innermost part of the human soul. We might say that the temple in which Philo seeks initiation <term>Initiation: A transformative process of spiritual training through which a person is "admitted" into a direct experience of divine reality.</term> is located entirely within himself, and his higher experiences constitute his own version of the Mysteries. In his case, processes of a purely spiritual nature replace the physical initiatory ceremonies once performed in sacred sanctuaries.

<summary>This page explores how early non-Christian thought, led by Philo of Alexandria, shifted the focus of spiritual "initiation" from external rituals to internal experiences. This transition represents a bridge between Greek philosophy and the developing Christian mystical tradition.</summary>

<keywords>Philo of Alexandria, Platonic philosophy, initiation, internal mysteries, spiritualization, Greco-Roman culture, soul-development</keywords>

--- Page 234 ---
<meta>This page continues the exploration of Philo of Alexandria’s philosophy, specifically focusing on the transition from intellectual knowledge to a direct, mystical experience of the divine. It follows the previous page's discussion on moving the "Mysteries" into the inner soul.</meta>

# Christianity as Mystical Fact

According to Philo <note>Philo of Alexandria (c. 20 BCE – 50 CE) was a Hellenistic Jewish philosopher who bridged the gap between Greek philosophy and the wisdom of the Hebrew scriptures.</note>, sense-observation and knowledge gained through the logical intellect do not lead to the divine. They have merely to do with what is **perishable**. But there is a way by which the soul may rise above these methods. It must come out of what it calls its ordinary self: from this it must withdraw. Then it enters a state of spiritual exaltation and **illumination**, in which it no longer knows, thinks, and judges in the ordinary sense of the words; for it has become merged, identified with the divine, which is experienced in its essence, and cannot be imparted in thought-concepts or abstract ideas.

It is experienced, and one who goes through this experience knows that no one can impart it, for the only way of reaching it is to live it. The visible world is an image of this mystic reality which is experienced in the inmost recesses of the soul.

The world has come forth from the invisible, inconceivable God. The harmony of the <term>Cosmos: The universe viewed as a complex, ordered, and harmonious system, often seen as a reflection of divine wisdom.</term>, which is steeped in wisdom, and to which sense-phenomena are subject, is a direct reflection of the Godhead, its spiritual image. It is divine spirit poured out into the

<summary>Philo explains that divine truth is inaccessible via logic or the senses, requiring instead a direct, lived experience of spiritual illumination. He characterizes the physical world as a harmonious reflection of an invisible, inconceivable God.</summary>

The person who has developed spirituality is the vehicle of the Logos. Above the Logos is God; beneath is the perishable world. It is man’s vocation to form the link between the two. He thus describes the inner actions of the soul: the wisdom in man's inner being walks along, "tracing the paths of the Father, and shapes the forms while beholding the <term>archetypes: In Platonic and Philonic philosophy, these are the original, eternal patterns or "blueprints" of which all things in the physical world are merely imperfect copies.</term>." This is in harmony with the interpretation of the myths of the people in the light of the <term>Mysteries: Sacred rituals and secret teachings of the ancient world intended to provide the initiate with direct knowledge of the divine and the immortality of the soul.</term>. ### Christianity as Mystical Fact

It is evident that this vision of the world is very similar to that of Christianity. Believers of the community of Jesus said: "That which was from the beginning, which we have heard, which we have seen with our eyes, which we have looked upon, and our hands have handled, of the **Word of life** <note>The author is quoting the First Epistle of John (1 John 1:1), where the apostle emphasizes that the divine "Word" was a physical reality they could touch and see.</note> . . . declare we unto you." In the same way it might be said in the spirit of **Neo-Platonism**, "That which was from the beginning, which cannot be heard and seen, must be spiritually experienced as the **Word of life**."

And so the old conception of the universe is developed and splits into two leading ideas. It leads in Neo-Platonism and similar systems to an idea of **Christ** which is purely spiritual; on the other hand, it leads to a fusion of the idea of Christ with a historical manifestation, the personality of **Jesus**. The writer of the **Gospel of St. John** may be said to unite these two conceptions. "In the beginning was the **Word** <term>Word: This translates the Greek 'Logos,' a term used by both Greek philosophers and early Christians to describe the divine logic or reason that created and sustains the universe.</term>." He shares this conviction with the Neo-Platonists. The Word becomes spirit within the soul, thus do the Neo-Platonists conclude. The Word was made flesh in Jesus, thus does St. John conclude.

The **Logos** become man had to go through it in a way that made this fact one that is true for or valid to the whole of humanity. Something which was present under the old dispensation as an incident in the **Mysteries** <note>The "Mysteries" were the secret religious rites of the ancient world (such as the Eleusinian or Orphic Mysteries) where initiates were taught the secrets of life, death, and the soul.</note> becomes a historical fact through Christianity. Hence Christianity was the fulfilment not only of what the Jewish prophets had predicted, but also of the truth which had been prefigured in the Mysteries.

## XIII

### ST. AUGUSTINE AND THE CHURCH

The full force of the conflict which took place within the souls of Christian believers during the transition from **paganism** <note>In this context, "paganism" refers to the diverse religious and philosophical traditions of the Greco-Roman world that existed before the widespread adoption of Christianity.</note> to the new religion is displayed in the person of **St. Augustine** (A.D. 354-430). The spiritual struggles of **Origen**, **Clement of Alexandria**, **Gregory Nazianzen**, **Jerome**, and others <note>These figures are known as the "Church Fathers." They were early Christian theologians who worked to define the doctrines of the Church, often by reconciling Greek philosophy with Christian faith.</note> are full of mysterious interest when we see them calmed and laid to rest in the mind of Augustine.

In Augustine’s personality, deep spiritual needs developed out of a passionate nature. He passed through pagan and semi-Christian ideas. He suffered deeply from the most appalling doubts of the kind which attack one who has felt the impotence of many varieties of thought in the face of spiritual problems, and who has tasted the depressing

effect of the question: "Can man know anything whatever?"

At the beginning of his struggles, Augustine's thoughts clung to the perishable things of sense. <note>The "things of sense" refer to the physical, material world experienced through sight, touch, and sound, which Augustine initially believed was the only reality.</note> He could only picture the spiritual to himself in material images. It is a deliverance for him when he rises above this stage. He thus describes it in his **Confessions** <note>original Latin: "Confessiones." This autobiographical work, written around 397–400 AD, outlines his sinful youth and his eventual conversion to Christianity.</note>:

> "When I wished to think of God, I could only imagine immense masses of bodies and believed that was the only kind of thing that could exist. This was the chief and almost the only cause of the errors which I could not avoid."

He thus indicates the point at which a person must arrive who is seeking the **true life of the spirit**. There are thinkers, not a few, who maintain that it is impossible to arrive at **pure thought** <term>pure thought: A state of meditation or cognition where the mind is no longer reliant on physical pictures or sensory data to understand truth.</term>, free from any material admixture. These thinkers confuse what they feel bound to say about their own inner life with what is humanly possible. The truth rather is that it is only possible to arrive at higher knowledge when thought has been liberated from all material things, when an inner life has been developed in which images of reality do not cease when

...their demonstration in sense-impressions comes to an end. Augustine relates how he attained to **spiritual vision**. Everywhere he asked where the divine was to be found. “I asked the earth and she said ‘I am not it’ and all that was upon the earth said the same. I asked the ocean and the abysses and all that lives in them, which said, ‘We are not thy God, seek beyond us.’ I asked the winds, and the whole atmosphere and its inhabitants said, ‘The philosophers who sought for the essence of things in us were under an illusion, we are not God.’ I asked the sun, moon, and stars, which said, ‘We are not God whom thou seekest.’” <note>original Latin: "Interrogavi terram, et dixit: 'Non sum'; et quaecumque in eadem sunt, idem confessa sunt..." This passage from Book X of the *Confessions* illustrates Augustine's "ascent" of the mind, where he questions the physical world only to be directed inward toward the soul.</note>

And it came home to St. Augustine that there is only one thing which can answer his question about the divine—his own soul. The soul said, “No eyes nor ears can impart to thee what is in me. For I alone can tell thee, and I tell thee in an un-questionable way.” “Men may be doubtful whether **vital force** <term>vital force: Often referring to the "anima" or soul-principle, which early thinkers debated was made of physical elements like air (pneuma) or fire.</term> is situate in air or in fire, but who can doubt that he himself lives, remembers, understands, wills, thinks, knows, and judges? If he doubts, it is a proof that he is alive, he remembers why he doubts, he... <note>This is a precursor to the famous "Cogito, ergo sum" (I think, therefore I am). Augustine argues in works like *The City of God* (original: "De Civitate Dei") and *On the Trinity* (original: "De Trinitate") that even the act of doubting is an act of a living, thinking being, thereby providing a foundation of absolute certainty that the physical world cannot provide.</note>

<summary>This page recounts St. Augustine's poetic search for God in the natural world, concluding that the divine can only be found within the human soul. It introduces his foundational argument that the certainty of one's own consciousness and the act of doubting prove the existence of the self.</summary>

He comes by degrees to the conviction that “the law of this tradition, which consists in believing what it has not proved, is moderate and without guile.” <note>Augustine argues here that faith in tradition is a necessary starting point for understanding; we must often believe in the authority of the community before we can achieve full intellectual proof.</note> He arrives at the idea, “Who could be so blind as to say that the Church of the Apostles deserves to have no faith placed in it, when it is so loyal and is supported by the conformity of so many brethren; when these have handed down their writings to posterity so conscientiously, and when the Church has so strictly maintained the succession of teachers, down to our present bishops?” <note>This refers to the concept of "Apostolic Succession"—the claim that the authority of the Church is validated by an unbroken chain of leadership reaching back to the original twelve Apostles.</note>

Thomas Aquinas <note>An Italian Dominican friar and philosopher (1225–1274) who became the preeminent spokesperson for Catholic theology, blending Aristotelian logic with Christian faith.</note>, the greatest teacher within the Church (1224–1274), has set forth this doctrine in his writings in a variety of ways. The nature of the divine and its relation to the world is given by <term>revealed theology</term> <note>Theology based on divine revelation, such as scripture and tradition, rather than on logic or observation of the natural world.</note>, which is not accessible to man’s own researches and is, as the substance of faith, superior to all knowledge.

### St. Augustine and the Church

The ancient Mysteries <note>Secret religious traditions of the ancient world, such as those at Eleusis, where initiates were given hidden knowledge about life and death.</note> withheld from the multitude became, when presented in this way by Christian thought and feeling, the content of faith, which by its very nature had to do with something unattainable by mere knowledge. The conviction of the pre-Christian Mystic <note>A seeker who pursued direct, personal experience of the divine through secret rites or meditation.</note> was that to him was given knowledge of the divine, while the people were obliged to have faith in its expression in images. 

Christianity came to the conviction that God has given his wisdom to mankind through revelation <note>The communication of truth from God to humanity, often through scripture or divine influence, which is accepted as true even if it cannot be fully proven by logic.</note>, and man attains through his knowledge an image of this divine revelation. The wisdom of the Mysteries is a hothouse plant <note>A metaphor for something delicate and exclusive that can only survive in a controlled, private environment.</note>, which is revealed to a few individuals ripe for it. Christian wisdom is a Mystery revealed as knowledge to none, but as a content of faith revealed to all. The standpoint of the Mysteries lived on in Christianity, but in a different form. All, not only the special individual, were to share in the truth, but the process was that at a certain point man owned his inability to penetrate farther by means of knowledge, and thence ascended to faith. <meta>This page provides supplementary notes to the main text, specifically clarifying the author's stance on natural science and historical biblical research. It continues from the previous discussion on the transition of the ancient Mysteries into the universal faith of Christianity, explaining how the author's spiritual perspective relates to modern scientific and historical methods.</meta>

### NOTES

P. 5—To one who has true perception, the **"Spirit of Nature"** <note>The animating intelligence or spiritual essence that the author suggests underlies the physical world, often referred to in German as the *Weltgeist* or *Naturgeist*.</note> speaks powerfully in the facts currently expressed by the catchword, **"struggle for existence,"** <note>A central concept in Darwinian evolutionary theory, describing the competition between organisms to survive and reproduce.</note> etc.; but not in the opinions which modern science deduces from them. In the first statement lies the reason why natural science is attracting more and more widespread attention. But it follows from the second statement that scientific opinions should not be taken as if they necessarily belonged to a knowledge of facts. The possibility of being led astray by mere opinion is, in these days, infinitely great.

P. 9—It should not be concluded from these remarks about the sources of **St. Luke’s Gospel**, <note>One of the four accounts of Jesus' life in the New Testament. The author implies that while historical study of these texts is valuable, a spiritual perspective offers deeper insights that do not contradict proven history.</note> that purely historical research is undervalued by the writer of this book. This is not the case. Historical research is absolutely justified, but it should not be impatient with the method of presentation proceeding from a **spiritual point of view**. <term>Spiritual point of view (Geisteswissenschaft)</term> <note>Also known as "Spiritual Science," this refers to a systematic, research-oriented approach to studying spiritual realities with the same rigor one applies to the natural world.</note> It is not considered of importance to make various kinds of quotations in this book; but one who is willing will be able to see that a really unprejudiced, broad-minded judgment will not find anything that is here stated to be contrary to what has been actually and historically proved. One who will not be broad-minded, but who holds this or that theory to be a firmly-established fact, may easily think that assertions made in this book are untenable from a scientific point of view, and are made without any objective foundation.

### NOTES

P. 15—It is said above that those whose **spiritual eyes** <note>A metaphor for a higher faculty of perception that allows a person to experience realities beyond the physical senses.</note> are opened are able to see into the **spiritual world**. The conclusion must not on this account be drawn that only one who possesses spiritual sight is able to form an intelligent opinion about the results arrived at by the **initiate** <note>A person who has undergone specific training and rituals to gain direct knowledge of spiritual truths.</note>. Spiritual sight belongs only to the investigator. If he afterwards communicates what he has discovered, every one can understand it who gives fair play to his reason and preserves an unbiased sense of truth. And such an one may also apply the results of research to life and derive satisfaction from them without himself having spiritual sight.

P. 20—“The **sinking into the mire**” <note>A reference to Plato’s *Phaedo*, where he suggests that those who arrive in the afterlife without having been initiated into the Mysteries will lie in filth or mud, representing the stagnation of a soul tied only to material desires.</note> spoken of by **Plato** must also be interpreted in the sense referred to in the last note.

P. 20—What is said about the impossibility of imparting the teaching of the **Mysteries** <note>Secret religious rites of ancient Greece and Rome, such as the Eleusinian Mysteries, which promised participants a better fate in the afterlife.</note> has reference to the fact that they could not be communicated to those unprepared in the same form in which the initiate experienced them; but they were always communicated to those outside in such a form as was possible for the uninitiated to understand. For instance the **myths** gave the old form, in order to communicate the content of the Mysteries in a way that was generally comprehensible.

P. 88—Everything that relates to knowledge gained through the “eyes of the spirit” is called by ancient mysticism “<term>Mantik</term>.” <note>original: "Mantik". From the Greek *mantikē*, usually meaning divination or the art of the seer.</note> “<term>Telestik</term>,” <note>original: "Telestik". From the Greek *telestikē*, referring to the rites, purifications, and consecrations used to achieve spiritual perfection.</note> on the other hand, is the indication of the ways which lead to initiation.

P. 168—“<term>Kabirs</term>,” <note>original: "Kabirs". Also known as the *Cabeiri*, these were a group of enigmatic deities worshipped in ancient Samothrace and elsewhere; the author views them as symbols of higher evolutionary stages of consciousness.</note> according to ancient mysticism, are beings with a consciousness far above the human consciousness of to-day. <term>Schelling</term> <note>Friedrich Wilhelm Joseph von Schelling (1775–1854), a prominent German philosopher who explored the relationship between nature, art, and the spiritual "Absolute."</note> means that man through initiation ascends to a state of consciousness above his present one.

and only adequate greeting for him <note>The author refers here to the Divine or the Absolute Being, often associated with the Greek "Eì" (Thou art).</note> is to declare that he **is**. In that existence we really have no part here, for every mortal being, situated between birth and destruction, merely manifests an appearance, a feeble and uncertain image of itself. 

If we try to grasp it with our understanding, it is as when water is tightly compressed and runs over merely through the pressure, spoiling what it touches. For the understanding, pursuing a too definite conception of each being that is subject to accidents and change, loses its way, now in the origin of the being, now in its destruction, and is unable to apprehend anything lasting or really existing. 

For, as <term>Heraclitus<note>Heraclitus of Ephesus (c. 535 – 475 BCE) was a pre-Socratic Greek philosopher known for his belief that change is the fundamental essence of the universe.</note></term> says, we cannot swim twice in the same wave <note>original: "ποταμῷ οὐκ ἔστιν ἐμβῆναι δὶς τῷ αὐτῷ" — usually translated as "No man ever steps in the same river twice."</note>, neither can we lay hold of a mortal being twice in the same state, for, through the violence and rapidity of movement, it is destroyed and recomposed; it comes into being and again decays; it comes and goes. 

Therefore, that which is <term>becoming<note>In philosophy, "Becoming" refers to the constant change and movement of the physical world, contrasted with "Being," which is eternal and unchanging.</note></term> can neither attain real existence, because growth neither ceases nor pauses. Change begins in the germ, and forms an **embryo**; then there appears a child, then a youth, a man, and an

<summary>This page explores the philosophical tension between the eternal state of "Being" and the transient state of "Becoming," using Heraclitus’s famous river analogy to illustrate the fluidity of human existence.</summary>

# Christianity as Mystical Fact

and only adequate greeting for him <note>Referring to the Divine or the "One." This passage draws from the philosopher Plutarch’s essay on the word "EI" (meaning "Thou Art") inscribed at the Temple of Apollo in Delphi.</note> is to declare that he **is**. In that existence we really have no part here, for every mortal being, situated between birth and destruction, merely manifests an appearance, a feeble and uncertain image of itself. 

If we try to grasp it with our understanding, it is as when water is tightly compressed and runs over merely through the pressure, spoiling what it touches. For the understanding, pursuing a too definite conception of each being that is subject to <term>accidents<note>In classical logic and philosophy, "accidents" refer to qualities that are not essential to the nature of a thing (like its color or size) and are subject to change, as opposed to its unchanging "essence."</note></term> and change, loses its way, now in the origin of the being, now in its destruction, and is unable to apprehend anything lasting or really existing. 

For, as <term>Heraclitus<note>A pre-Socratic Greek philosopher (c. 535–475 BCE) famous for the doctrine that change is central to the universe and that "all things flow."</note></term> says, we cannot swim twice in the same wave <note>original Greek: "ποταμῷ οὐκ ἔστιν ἐμβῆναι δὶς τῷ αὐτῷ." This is the famous fragment often translated as "No man ever steps in the same river twice."</note>, neither can we lay hold of a mortal being twice in the same state, for, through the violence and rapidity of movement, it is destroyed and recomposed; it comes into being and again decays; it comes and goes. 

Therefore, that which is <term>becoming<note>The philosophical state of being in flux or in the process of change, rather than having a fixed, eternal existence.</note></term> can neither attain real existence, because growth neither ceases nor pauses. Change begins in the germ, and forms an embryo; then there appears a child, then a youth, a man, and an
